         j.=jl we;s fma<s wkkHj y÷kd.; yels ;Srejls ^fyda ;Sre lÜg,hls&'
     •   First Normal Form / m<uq m%u:lrK wjia:dj (1NF)
         A table where each column contains atomic values, meaning no repeating groups or arrays.
         tla tla ;Srefjys mrudKql w.hka wvx.= j.=jls" tkï mqkrdj¾;k lKavdhï fyda wrdjka fkdue;'
     •   Second Normal Form / fojk m%u:lrK wjia:dj (2NF)
         A table that is in 1NF and every non-key attribute is fully dependent on the whole primary key.
         1NF ys we;s j.=jla iy iEu h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla iïmQ¾K uQ,sl h;=r u; iïmQ¾Kfhkau
         r|d mj;S'
     •   Cardinality / .Kksh;djh
         The number of rows in a table.
         j.=jl we;s fma<s .Kk'

     Now, let's review each option. / oeka" tla tla úl,amh iudf,dapkh lruq'

         (StudentNo, SubjectID) u; fkdfõ' tneúka fuu m%ldYh idjoH fõ'

     2) It has one candidate key / thg tla ksrEmH (candidate key) h;=rla we;'
         The StudentNo and SubjectID combination may serve as a composite primary key, but

 4. Which dependency is removed when converting the Results table to next normal form?
    Results j.=j B,Õ m%u;lrKhg m;alrk úg my; l=uk mrdh;a;;dj (dependency) bj;a fõ o?

       wd.ka;=l h;=re (foreign key) mrdh;a;;dj

    4) partial dependencies of non-key attributes on the primary key

    Important concepts / jeo.;a ixl,am

    •   Foreign key dependency / wd.ka;=l h;=re mrdhla;;d
        Refers to a relationship between two tables based on foreign keys.
        wd.ka;=l h;=re u; mokï jQ j.= folla w;r iïnkaOhla .ek i|yka lrhs'

    •   Fully functional dependency / mq¾K ld¾hnoaO mrdhla;;d
        A non-key attribute depends on the entire primary key.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla iïmQ¾K m%d:ñl h;=r u; r|d mj;S'

    •   Multivalued dependency / nyqw.hka iys; mrdhla;;d
        Occurs when a non-key attribute depends on part of the primary key, but not the whole key.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla m%d:ñl h;=f¾ fldgila u; rojd mj;sk úg isÿ, iïmq¾K h;=r u;
        fkdfõ'

    •   Partial dependency / w¾O mrdhla;;d
        Occurs when a non-key attribute is dependent only on part of a composite key, rather than the
        entire key. Removing partial dependencies brings the table from 1NF to 2NF.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla iïmQ¾K h;=rg jvd ixhqla; h;=rl fldgila u; muKla r|d mj;sk
        úg isÿ fõ' w¾O mrdh;a;;d bj;a lsÍu j.=j 1NF isg 2NF olajd f.k ths'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                             -48-                                       #ictfromabc


    •   Transitive dependency / ixl%dka;s mrdhla;;d

        dependencies brings the table to 3NF.
        h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla fjk;a h;=re fkdjk Wm,laIKhla u; r|d mj;sk úg isÿ fõ'
        ixl%dka;s mrdh;a;;d bj;a lsÍu j.=j 3NF fj; f.k ths'

    1) Foreign key dependency

 5. Following are the steps involved in creating an Entity Relationship (ER) Diagram:
    N+;d¾: iïnkaO;d (ER) rE igykla ks¾udKh lsÍug wod< mshjr my; oelafõ'

      I. Determine the .....A..... in your diagram.
         Tnf.a rE igykg wjYH .....A..... ;SrKh lrkak'

     II. Add ....B.... to each ....C....
         ....B.... tla tla ....C.... g tl;= lrkak'

    III. Include the ....D.... between the ....A....
         ....A.... w;r ....D.... tl;= lrkak'

    IV. Add ....E.... to every relationship.
        iEu iïnkaO;djhlgu .....E.... o tla lrkak'

    Which of the following gives suitable choices for the A, B, C, D and E blanks in the above steps?
    by; A, B, C, D iy E ysia;eka i|yd ksjerÈ f;dard.ekSï olajkafka my; ljrla o?

    1) A - Wm,els (attributes), B - N+;d¾: (entities), C - Wm,elsh (attribute), D - .KkSh;dj (cardinality),
       E - N+;d¾: (entities)
    2) A - Wm,els (attributes), B - .KkSh;dj (cardinality), C - Wm,elsh (attributes), D - N+;d¾:
       (entities), E - N+;d¾: (entities)
    3) A - N+;d¾: (entities), B - Wm,els (attributes), C - N+;d¾:h (entities), D - iïnkaO;d (relationships),
       E - .KkSh;dj (cardinality)
    4) A - N+;d¾: (entities), B - iïnkaO;d (relationships), C - N+;d¾:h (entities), D - Wm,els (attributes),
       E - .KkSh;dj (cardinality)
    5) A - iïnkaO;d, B - .KkSh;dj (cardinality), C - iïnkaO;djh, D - Wm,els (attributes),
       E - N+;d¾: (entities)

                                                                                                      ( 2021 – 36 )
    Answer : 3 )
    In an Entity-Relationship (ER) diagram, the core elements include entities, attributes, relationships, and
    cardinality. These concepts help to visually represent the data structure and relationships in a database
    system.
    N+;d¾;-iïnkaO;d ^ER& igykl" uQ,sl uQ,dx.j,g N+;d¾;" Wm,laIK" iïnkaO;d iy .KkSh;dj
    we;=<;a fõ' fuu ixl,am o;a; iuqod moaO;shl o;a; jHqyh iy iïnkaO;d oDYH f,i ksrEmKh
    lsÍug WmldÍ fõ'
    Entities / N+;d¾;
    Entities represent real-world objects or concepts that have a distinct existence in the system. They are
    typically things or objects that need to be stored in a database.
    N+;d¾; u.ska moaO;sh ;=< meyeÈ,s meje;aula we;s ienE f,dalfha jia;+ka fyda ixl,am ksfhdackh
    lrhs' tAjd idudkHfhka o;a; .nvdjl .nvd l< hq;= foaj,a fyda jia;= fõ'
    Attributes / Wm,els
    Attributes are the properties or characteristics of an entity. They define the details or qualities of an entity
    that need to be stored in the database.
    Wm,els hkq jia;=jl .=Kdx. fyda ,laIK fõ' o;a; .nvdfõ .nvd l< hq;= N+;d¾;hl úia;r fyda
    .=Kdx. Tjqka ks¾jpkh lrhs'
    Relationships / iïnkaO;d
    Relationships represent the associations or interactions between two or more entities. They show how
    entities are related to each other.
    iïnkaO;d u.ska N+;d¾; folla fyda jeä .Kkla w;r we;s iïnkaOlï fyda wka;¾l%shd ksfhdackh
    lrhs' N+;d¾; tlsfkl iïnkaO jk wdldrh tAjd fmkajhs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -50-                                           #ictfromabc

    Cardinality / .KkSh;dj
    Cardinality defines the numerical relationship between entities. It specifies how many instances of one
    entity can be associated with instances of another entity.
    .KkSh;dj N+;d¾; w;r ixLHd;aul iïnkaO;djh ks¾jpkh lrhs' th tla N+;d¾;hl wjia:d lShla
    ;j;a N+;d¾;hl wjia:d iuÕ iïnkaO l< yelso hkak i|yka lrhs'
    The steps to create this Entity-Relationship (ER) diagram are as follows.
    fuu N+;d¾; iïnkaO;d igykla ks¾udKh lsÍfï mshjr my; mßÈ fõ'
    1 - The first step in creating an ER diagram is to determine the entities (real-world objects or concepts)
        that will be represented in the diagram.
        ER igykla ks¾udKh lsÍfï m<uq mshjr jkafka igyfkys ksrEmKh jk N+;d¾; ^ienE f,dal
        jia;= fyda ixl,am& ;SrKh lsÍuhs'
    2 - Each entity is characterized by attributes (details or properties of the entity). The entities are the core
        components of an ER diagram, so each attribute is added to an entity.
        iEu N+;d¾;hlau Wm,els j,ska ix,laIs; fõ ^N+;d¾;fha úia;r fyda .=Kdx.&' N+;d¾; hkq ER
        rEm igykl uQ,sl fldgia fõ" tneúka tla tla Wm,elsh" N+;d¾;hlg tl;= fõ'
    3 - After defining the entities, the next step is to include the relationships between them, which represent
        how entities interact with each other.
        N+;d¾; ks¾jpkh lsÍfuka miq" B<Õ mshjr jkafka tAjd w;r iïnkaO;d we;=<;a lsÍuhs" th
        N+;d¾; tlsfkld iuÕ wka;¾ l%shd lrk wdldrh ksfhdackh lrhs'
    4 - Cardinality defines the number of occurrences of one entity that can be associated with the number
        of occurrences of another entity in a relationship.
        .KkSh;dj u.ska iïnkaO;djhl" tla N+;d¾;hl isÿùï .Kk iuÕ iïnkaO l< yels ;j;a
        N+;d¾;hl isÿùï .Kk ks¾jpkh lrhs'
    Accordingly, the answers are given below.
    tA wkqj my; wdldrhg ms<s;=re ,efí'

    A – entities / N+;d¾;                      B – attributes / Wm,els                    C – entity / N+;d¾;h
    D – relationships / iïnkaO;d               E – cardinality / .KkSh;dj
    So, the correct answer number is 3.
    tuksid, ksjerÈ ms<s;=re wxlh 3 fõ'

 6. Which of the following can be modelled with an Extended Entity Relationship diagram?
    úia;D; N+;d¾: iïnkaO;d (Extended Entity Relationship) rE igyklska my; ljrla ksrEmKh l<
    yels o?
    A - subclasses of an entity N+;d¾:hl Wmmka;s
    B - inheritance of attributes Wm,els Wreuùu
    C - specialization of entities N+;d¾:j, úfYaIdhkh

    1) A only.           2) B only.           3) C only.           4) A and C only.     5) All of the above.
       A muKs.              B muKs.              C muKs.              A yd C muKs.         by; ish,a,u.

                                                                                                   ( 2021 – 37 )
    Answer : 5 )

    An Enhanced Entity-Relationship (EER) diagram is a visual representation of a database schema that
    extends the traditional Entity-Relationship (ER) model to capture more complex relationships and data
    structures. It's a valuable tool for database design, especially when dealing with intricate systems.
    úia;D; N+;d¾;-iïnkaO;d ^EER& igykla hkq jvd;a ixlS¾K iïnkaO;d iy o;a; jHqyhka .%yKh
    lr .ekSu i|yd iïm%odhsl N+;d¾;-iïnkaO;d ^ER& wdlD;sh È.= lrk o;a; iuqod l%uhl oDYH
    ksrEmKhls' th úfYaIfhka ixlS¾K moaO;s iuÕ lghq;= lrk úg" o;a; iuqod ks¾udKh i|yd jákd
    fuj,uls'

    Subclasses of an entity / N+;d¾:hl Wmmka;s
    Subclasses represent a more specific version of an entity. For example, if you have an entity called Person,
    subclasses could be Student and Teacher. These subclasses inherit attributes from the parent entity Person
    (e.g., name, age), but may also have their own unique attributes (e.g., grade for Student, subject for
    Teacher). In EER subclasses allow more detailed representation of entities with variations in behavior or
    characteristics.
    Wmmka;s hkq N+;d¾;hl jvd;a ksYaÑ; wkqjdohls' WodyrKhla f,i" Tng mqoa.,hd kñka N+;d¾;hla
    ;sfí kï" Wmmka;s YsIH iy .=re úh yel' fuu Wmmka;sj,g m%Odk N+;d¾;h jk mqoa.,hdf.ka
    .=Kdx. ^Wod' ku" jhi& Wreu fõ" kuq;a Tjqkagu wkkH .=Kdx. o ;sìh yelsh ^Wod( YsIH i|yd
    fY%aKsh" .=rejrhd i|yd úIh&' EER Wmmka;sj, yeisÍï fyda ,laIKj, fjkialï iys; N+;d¾;
    jvd;a iúia;rd;aulj ksrEmKh lsÍug bv i,ihs'

    Inheritance of attributes / Wm,els Wreuùu
    Inheritance refers to the concept where a subclass inherits attributes from its parent entity. For example, a
    Teacher (subclass) inherits common attributes like name and age from the Person (parent entity) but may
    also have additional attributes like subject. In EER inheritance allows for sharing of common attributes
    across related entities, reducing redundancy.
    Wreuh hkafkka woyia lrkafka Wmmka;shlg tys m%Odk N+;d¾;fhka .=Kdx. Wreu jk ixl,amhhs'
    WodyrKhla f,i" .=rejrfhl=g ^Wm mka;shg& mqoa.,hdf.ka ^m%Odk N+;d¾;fhka& ku iy jhi jeks
    fmdÿ .=Kdx. Wreu jk kuq;a úIh jeks wu;r .=Kdx. o ;sìh yel' EER Wreuh ;=<" w;sßla;h
    wvq lrñka wod< N+;d¾; yryd fmdÿ .=Kdx. fnod .ekSug bv i,ihs'

    Specialization of entities / N+;d¾:j, úfYaIdhkh
    Specialization refers to the process of defining a more specific entity from a general one. For instance,
    Vehicle can be specialized into Car and Truck. Specialization defines entities that are more specific
    versions of a generalized entity. In EER specialization is used to model how entities can be divided into
    distinct, specialized groups based on shared attributes or behaviors.
    úfYaISlrKh hkq idudkH tllska jvd;a ksYaÑ; jia;=jla ks¾jpkh lsÍfï l%shdj,shhs' WodyrKhla
    f,i" jdykh" ld¾ iy g%la i|yd úfYaIs; úh yel' úfYaISlrKh u.ska idudkHlrKh jQ N+;d¾;hl
    jvd;a ksYaÑ; wkqjdo jk N+;d¾; ks¾jpkh lrhs' EER úfYaISlrKh ;=< yjq,a .=Kdx. fyda yeisÍï
    u; mokïj N+;d¾; fjkia" úfYaIs; lKavdhïj,g fnÈh yels wdldrh wdo¾Ykh lsÍug Ndú;d
    lrhs'

    Accordingly all these can be represented in an EER diagram. So, the correct answer number is 5.
    tA wkqj fuu ish,a,u EER igykl ksrEmKh l< yelsh' tuksid, ksjerÈ ms<s;=re wxlh 5 fõ'

  2. Which of the following shows the most suitable primary keys for Order and Order_Product relations?
     Order iy Order_Product j.= i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq m%d:ñl h;=re ms<sfj<ska oelafjkafka my; ljrl o?

      1)   Order: CusId,                Order_ Product: OrderId
      2)   Order: OrderId,              Order_Product: OrderId
      3)   Order: OrderId,              Order_Product: OrderId + ProdId
      4)   Order: CusId + SellerId,     Order Product: ProdId
      5)   Order: OrderId + CusId,      Order_Product: OrderId

                                                                                                    ( 2021 – 30 )
      Answer : 3 )

      1. Order Table contains OrderId, CusId, OrderDate, and SellerId.
         Order j.=fõ OrderId, CusId, OrderDate, iy SellerId wvx.= fõ
           OrderId should uniquely identify each record since each order should have a unique identifier.
           iEu weKjqulgu wkkH y÷kd.ekSula ;sìh hq;= neúka OrderId iEu jd¾;djlau wkkH f,i
           y÷kd.; hq;=h'

      2. Order_Product Table is a junction table that connects Order and Product tables and contains
         OrderId and ProdId.
         Order_Product Table hkq weKjqï iy ksIamdok j.= iïnkaO lrk ikaê j.=jla jk w;r
         OrderId iy ProdId wvx.= fõ'
           A combination of OrderId and ProdId is typically used to uniquely identify each record in a junction
           table like this because it defines which products are associated with which orders. A single order can
           contain multiple different products, and the same product can appear in multiple orders.
           OrderId iy ProdId ixfhda.hla idudkHfhka fujeks ikaê j.=jl tla tla jd¾;dj wkkH f,i
           y÷kd .ekSug Ndú;d lrkqfha th l=uk weKjqï iuÕ iïnkaO ù we;ao hkak ks¾jpkh lrk
           neúks' ;ks weKjqul úúO ksIamdok lsysmhla wvx.= úh yels w;r" tu NdKavh nyqúO weKjqïj,
           Èia úh yel'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                            -55-                                       #ictfromabc


     Given the options:
     ,nd § we;s úl,am j,ska(

     1) Order: CusId, Order_Product: OrderId
        Incorrect because CusId does not uniquely identify an order.
        CusId wkqms<sfj,la wkkH f,i y÷kd fkd.kakd ksid jerÈhs'

     2) Order: OrderId, Order_Product: OrderId

 3. Which of the following is correct regarding the Order relation?
    Order j.=j ie,lSfï§ my; i|yka l=ula ksjerÈ o?
    1) CusId attribute uniquely identifies each tuple in the relation.
       CusId Wm,laIKh (attribute) u.ska j.=fõ tla tla Wm,elshdk (tuple) wkkHj y÷kd.kS'
    2) The relation is in First Normal Form (1NF).
       j.=j tys m%:u m%u; wjia:dfõ (First Normal Form-1NF) mj;S'
    3) The relation is in Second Normal Form (2NF).
       j.=j tys fojk m%u; wjia:dfõ (Second Normal Form-2NF) mj;S'
    4) Orders of each customer are handled by a unique salesperson.
       tla tla mdßfNda.slhdf.a weKjqï yiqrejkq ,nkafka wkkH úl=Kqïlrefjl= úisks'
    5) The relation consists of a composite primary key.
       j.=j ixhqla; m%d:ñl h;=rlska (composite primary key) iukaú; fõ'

                                                                                                 ( 2021 – 32 )
    Answer : 3 )
    To determine if a table is in Second Normal Form, it must first meet the criteria for First Normal Form
    (1NF), and then it must satisfy an additional condition related to the way attributes depend on the key.
    j.=jla fojk idudkH fmdarufha ;sfí oehs ;SrKh lsÍu i|yd" th m%:ufhka m<uq idudkH fmdaruh
    (1NF) i|yd jk ks¾Kdhl imqrd,sh hq;= w;r" miqj th úfYaI .=Kdx. u; r|d mj;sk wdldrh
    iïnkaO wu;r fldkafoaishla imqrd,sh hq;=h'

    Criteria for 1NF include:
    1NF i|yd jk ks¾Kdhlj,g we;=<;a jkafka(
    •   No repeating groups or arrays: The table should only have atomic column values.
        mqkrdj¾;k lKavdhï fyda arrays fkdue;( j.=fõ wxY=uh ;Sre w.hka muKla ;sìh hq;=h'
    •   Unique rows: Each row should be uniquely identifiable.
        tAlSh fma<s( iEu fma<shlau wkkHj y÷kd.; yels úh hq;=h'

    Criteria for 2NF include:
    2NF i|yd jk ks¾Kdhlj,g we;=<;a jkafka(
    Elimination of partial dependency: Non-key attributes must depend on the whole of a primary key, not
    just part of a composite key. This means that in a table with a composite key, every non-key attribute
    should depend on all components of the key for its existence.
    wdxYsl mrdh;a;;dj bj;a lsÍu( Non-key attributes fkdjk .=Kdx. ixhqla; h;=rl fldgila muKla
    fkdj m%d:ñl h;=rl iuia;h u; r|d mej;sh hq;=h' fuhska woyia lrkafka ixhqla; h;=rla iys;
    j.=jl" iEu h;=re fkdjk .=Kdx.hlau tys meje;au i|yd h;=f¾ ish¨u ixrpl u; r|d mej;sh
    hq;= njhs'

    Analyzing the Order Table Schema
    Order Table úYaf,aIKh lsÍu
    The schema for the Order table, based on your descriptions, includes the following fields:
    úia;r u; mokïj" Order table i|yd my; lafIa;% we;=<;a fõ(

    OrderId         CusId           OrderDate        SellerId

    Step 1: Verify 1NF
    mshjr 1: 1NF ;yjqre lrkak
    Given that each field in the Order table contains only atomic values (single values in each column per
    row), and each row is uniquely identifiable (presumably by OrderId), the table meets the criteria for 1NF.
    weKjqï j.=fõ iEu lafIa;%hlu wvx.= jkafka mrudKql w.hka ^fma<shlg tla tla ;Srefõ ;ks
    w.hka&" iy tla tla fma<sh wkkHj y÷kd.; yels ^wkqudk jYfhka OrderId u.ska&" j.=j 1NF
    i|yd jk ks¾Kdhl imqrd,hs'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                               -57-                                          #ictfromabc


     Step 2: Establish the Primary Key
     mshjr ^2(& m%d:ñl h;=r ia:dmkh lrkak
     For 2NF analysis, we need to confirm the primary key. If OrderId is the primary key and uniquely
     identifies each record, this simplifies our analysis.
     2NF úYaf,aIKh i|yd" wms m%d:ñl h;=r ;yjqre l< hq;=h' OrderId m%d:ñl h;=r jk w;r iEu
     jd¾;djlau wkkH f,i y÷kd .kafka kï" fuh wmf.a úYaf,aIKh ir, lrhs'

     Step 3: Check for Partial Dependencies
     mshjr ^3(& wdxYsl mrdh;a;;dj i|yd mÍlaId lrkak
     To be in 2NF:
     2NF ys isàug(
     •   Each attribute in the table should fully depend on the primary key.
         j.=fõ we;s iEu .=Kdx.hlau m%d:ñl h;=r u; iïmQ¾Kfhkau r|d mej;sh hq;=h'
     •   There should be no attribute that depends only on part of the primary key in cases of composite keys.
         ixhqla; h;=re wjia:d j,§ m%d:ñl h;=f¾ fldgila u; muKla r|d mj;sk .=Kdx.hla fkd;sìh
         hq;=h'

     Dependency Analysis:
     mrdh;a;;d úYaf,aIKh(
     •   CusId: This field depends on OrderId as each order can be linked to one customer. The customer of
         an order cannot be determined partially by another subset; it’s entirely dependent on knowing the full
         OrderId.

     •   OrderDate and SellerId: Similarly, the date an order was placed and the seller handling the order

     Conclusion for 2NF
     2NF i|yd ks.ukh
     The Order table is in 2NF because:
     weKjqï j.=j 2NF ys we;s ksid(
     •   It is already in 1NF.
         th oekgu;a 1NF ys we;'
     •   It has a single primary key (OrderId), under which all other fields (non-key attributes) in the table

         anything less than the full OrderId.

     3) The relation is in Second Normal Form (2NF) is the correct answer. This is because the Order table
     is already in First Normal Form (1NF) and all non-key attributes (CusId, OrderDate, SellerId) are fully
     dependent on the primary key (OrderId), ensuring no partial dependency exists. Thus, it meets the criteria
     for 2NF.

  4. Which of the following statements is/are correct regarding the normalization concept?
     m%u;lrKh(normalization) ixl,amh iïnkaOfhka my; i|yka l=uk m%ldY$h ksjerÈ o?

     A - In first normal form, atomic attributes are removed from a relation.
         m<uq m%u; wjia:dfõ§ (1NF), j.=j ;=< we;s mrudKql Wm,laIK (atomic attribute) bj;a lrkq
         ,efí'
     B - In second normal form, partial dependency of attributes on the primary key are removed.
         fojk m%u; wjia:dfõ§ (2NF), m%d:ñl h;=r (primary key) u; Wm,laIKj, wdxYsl mrdh;a;;dj
         (partial dependency) bj;a lrkq ,efí'
     C - In third normal form, transitive dependency of attributes are removed.
         f;jk m%u; wjia:dfõ§ (3NF), Wm,laIKj, ixl%dka;s mrdh;a;;dj (transitive dependency)
         bj;a lrkq ,efí'

     1) B only.        2) A and B only.      3) A and C only.      4) B and C only.      5) All of the above.
        B muKs.           A yd B muKs.          A yd C muKs.          B yd C muKs.          by; ish,a,u.

                                                                                                    ( 2021 – 33 )
     Answer : 4 )
     A. In first normal form, atomic attributes are removed from a relation.
        m<uq m%u;lrKfhka" iïnkaO;djhlska wxY=uh .=Kdx. bj;a lrkq ,efí'
         This statement is incorrect as phrased. In first normal form (1NF), a table is in 1NF if it contains only
         atomic values, meaning each column in a table must contain indivisible values. It doesn't involve
         "removing atomic attributes".
         fuu m%ldYh jdlH LKavh f,i jerÈh' m<uq m%u;lrKfhka(1NF), j.=jla wxY=uh w.hka
         muKla wvx.= kï 1NF ys we;" tkï j.=jl we;s iEu ;Srejlu fkdfnÈh hq;= w.hka wvx.=
         úh hq;=h' thg “mrudKql .=Kdx. bj;a lsÍu” we;=<;a fkdfõ'
     B. In second normal form, partial dependency of attributes on the primary key are removed.
        fojk m%u;lrKfhka" m%d:ñl h;=r u; .=Kdx.j, w¾O hemSu bj;a lrkq ,efí'
         This statement is correct. A table is in second normal form (2NF) if it is in 1NF and all non-key
         attributes are fully functionally dependent on the primary key. This means removing any partial
         dependencies where some attributes depend only on part of a composite primary key.
         fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈhs' j.=jla 1NF ys ;sfí kï fojk m%u;lrKfhka (2NF) jk w;r ish¨u
         h;=re fkdjk .=Kdx. m%d:ñl h;=r u; iïmQ¾Kfhkau l%shdldÍj r|d mj;S' fuhska woyia lrkafka
         ixhqla; m%d:ñl h;=rl fldgila u; muKla r|d mj;sk iuyr .=Kdx. ´kEu wdxYsl mrdh;a;;d
         bj;a lsÍuhs'
     C. In third normal form, transitive dependency of attributes are removed.
        f;jk m%u;lrKfhka" .=Kdx.j, ixl%dka;s hemSu bj;a lrkq ,efí'
         This statement is also correct. A table is in third normal form (3NF) if it is in 2NF and no non-key
         attribute depends on other non-key attributes. In other words, it removes transitive dependencies where
         changes to non-key attributes could lead to inconsistencies.
         fï m%ldYh;a ksjerÈhs' j.=jla 2NF ys ;sfí kï ;=kajk m%u;lrKfhka (3NF) jk w;r lsisÿ
         h;=re-fkdjk (non-key) .=Kdx.hla fjk;a h;=re fkdjk(non-key) .=Kdx. u; r|d mj;S' fjk;a
         jpk j,ska lsjfyd;a" th m%Odk fkdjk .=Kdx.j, fjkiaùï fkd.e,mSï j,g ;=vq Èh yels
         ixl%dka;s mrdh;a;;d bj;a lrhs'
     Given the correct analysis of each statement:
     tla tla m%ldYh ksjerÈj úYaf,aIKh lsÍfuka(
         •   A is incorrect.
             A jerÈhs'
         •   B and C are correct.
             B iy C ksjerÈh
     So, the correct answer is 4).

     Answer : 3 )

     To address this question, we will evaluate each statement about Entity Relationship (ER) modeling to
     determine which are correct.
     fuu .eg¨j úi£u i|yd" wms N+;d¾: iïnkaO;d (ER) wdlD;s ks¾udKh ms<sn| tla tla m%ldYh
     ksjerÈ oehs ;SrKh lrkafkuq'

     A - A weak entity is dependent on another entity.
         ÿ¾j, N+;d¾;hla fjk;a N+;d¾:hla u; r|d mj;S'
         Correct. A weak entity does not have a sufficient set of attributes to form a primary key on its own and
         is dependent on some strong (or identifying) entity for its identity. Its existence and uniqueness are
         dependent on another entity with which it has an identifying relationship.
         ksjerÈh ÿ¾j, N+;d¾;hla ;ukaf.au m%d:ñl h;=rla iE§ug m%udKj;a .=Kdx. iuQyhla fkdue;s
         w;r tys wkkH;djh i|yd hï m%n, ^fyda y÷kd.ekSfï& N+;d¾:hla u; r|d mj;S' tys meje;au
         iy iqúfYaI;ajh r|d mj;skafka th y÷kd.ekSfï iïnkaO;djhla we;s fjk;a N+;d¾:hla u; h'

     B - A derived attribute is represented as an attribute in a relation.
         jHq;amkak lrk ,o Wm,laIKhla (derived attribute) j.=j ;=,§ Wm ,laIKhla f,i ksrEmKhfõ
         Wrong. In ER modeling, a derived attribute is one whose value is calculated from other attributes
         within the database. For example, an employee's age might be derived from their birthdate. While
         derived attributes are often shown in ER diagrams to illustrate these dependencies or calculations, they
         are generally not stored in relational tables as part of the physical database schema. Instead, their
         values are computed as needed.
         jerÈh. ER wdlD;slrKfha§" jHq;amkak Wm,laIKhla hkq o;a; iuqodh ;=< we;s wfkl=;a .=Kdx.
         j,ska w.h .Kkh lrkq ,nk tlls' ksoiqkla jYfhka" fiajlfhl=f.a jhi Tyqf.a Wmka Èkfhka
         ,nd .; yelsh' fuu mrdh;a;;d fyda .Kkh lsÍï kso¾Ykh lsÍu i|yd jHq;amkak Wm,laIK
         fndfyda úg ER rEm igyka j, fmkajd we;s w;r" tAjd idudkHfhka fN!;sl o;a; iuqod l%ufha
         fldgila f,i iïnkaOl j.= j, .nvd fkdfõ' tA fjkqjg" tAjdfha w.hka wjYH mßÈ .Kkh
         lrkq ,efí'

     C - An entity can contain a multi-value attribute and a composite attribute at the same time.
         jia;=jlg tljr nyq-w.h Wm,laIKhla iy ixhqla; .=Kdx.hla wvx.= úh yel'
         Correct. In ER modeling, an entity can indeed contain both multi-value and composite attributes. A
         multi-value attribute can store multiple values for a single attribute (like multiple phone numbers for
         a contact), while a composite attribute is one that can be subdivided into other attributes (like an
         address composed of street, city, state, and zip code).
         ksjerÈh. ER wdlD;s ks¾udKfha§" N+;d¾:hlg nyq-w.h iy ixhqla; .=Kdx. hk folu wvx.=
         úh yel' nyq-w.h Wm,laIKhg ;ks Wm,laIKhla i|yd nyqúO w.hka .nvd l< yel
         ^iïnkaO;djla i|yd ÿrl:k wxl lsysmhla jeks&" ixhqla; .=Kdx.hla hkq fjk;a Wm,laIK j,g
         fnÈh yels tlls ^ùÈh" k.rh" m%dka;h iy ;eme,a fla;hlska j,ska iukaú; ,smskhla jeks&'

     All statements A and C correctly describe aspects of ER modeling
     ish¨u m%ldY Aiy C wdlD;slrKfha wx. ksjerÈj úia;r lrhs

 2. When the Employee entity of the following

    To determine the correct matching between ER diagram components and the relational model, let's first
    understand the basic concepts.
    ER rEm igyka ixrpl iy iïnkaO;d (relational) wdlD;sh w;r ksjerÈ .e,mSu ;SrKh lsÍu i|yd"
    wms uq,skau uQ,sl ixl,am f;areï .ksuq'

    1. Entity: A real-world object or concept that can be distinctly identified. In the relational model, an
       entity corresponds to a table.
       meyeÈ,sj y÷kd.; yels ienE f,dal jia;=jla fyda ixl,amhla' iïnkaOl wdlD;sfha" jia;=jla
       j.=jlg wkqrEm fõ'
    2. Attribute: A property or characteristic of an entity. In the relational model, attributes correspond to
       fields (columns) in a table.
       N+;d¾;hl .=Kdx.hla fyda ,laIKhla iïnkaO;d wdlD;sfha" Wm,laIK j.=jl we;s lafIa;% ^;Sre&
       j,g wkqrEm fõ'
    3. Primary Key: A unique identifier for a record in a table.
       j.=jl jd¾;djla i|yd wkkH y÷kd.ekSula fõ'
    4. Unique Attribute: An attribute that must have unique values for each record.
       tla tla jd¾;dj i|yd wkkH w.hka ;sìh hq;= .=Kdx.hls'
    5. Multivalued Attribute: An attribute that can have multiple values for a single entity. In the relational
       model, multivalued attributes are often handled by creating a separate table.
       ;ks jia;=jla i|yd nyqúO w.hka ;sìh yels Wm,laIKhls' iïnkaO;d wdlD;sfha §" nyq w.hka
       iys; Wm,laIK fndfyda úg yiqrejkq ,nkafka fjku j.=jla ks¾udKh lsÍfuks'

    Let's analyze the given questions and statements with respect to the provided relations.
    ,nd § we;s iïnkaO;d iïnkaOfhka ,nd § we;s m%Yak iy m%ldY úYaf,aIKh lruq'
RAVINDU BANDARANAYAKE                                 -71-                                           #ictfromabc


    A. All relations are in 3rd normal form.
       ish¨ iïnkaO;d f;jeks m%u; wjia:dfõ (normal form) mj;S'
       To be in the 3rd normal form, a relation must be in the 2nd normal form and all its attributes must be
       non-transitively dependent on the primary key. Let’s verify each relation.
       3 jk idudkH iajrEmfhka (normal form) isàug" iïnkaO;djhla 2 jk m%u:lrK wjia:dfõ (normal
       form) ;sìh hq;= w;r tys ish¨ .=Kdx. primary h;=r u; ixl%dka;s fkdjk (non-transitively) f,i
       r|d mej;sh hq;=h' wms tla tla iïnkaO;djh ;yjqre lruq.

    •   adviser
        All attributes depend on adId (primary key).
        ish¨u Wm,laIK adId (primary h;=r& u; r|d mj;S'
    •   farmer
        All attributes depend on farmerId (primary key).
        ish¨u Wm,laIK farmerId (primary h;=r& u; r|d mj;S'
    •   task
        All attributes depend on taskId (primary key).
        ish¨u Wm,laIK taskId (primary h;=r& u; r|d mj;S'
    •   advises
        Composite primary key (adId, taskId), and all other attributes depend on this composite key.
        Thus, all relations appear to be in the 3rd normal form. Therefore, this statement is correct.
        ixhqla; m%d:ñl h;=r (adId, taskId) iy wfkl=;a ish¨u .=Kdx. fuu ixhqla; h;=r u; r|d
        mj;S' fï wkqj" ish¨ iïnkaO;d 3 jeks idudkH wdldrfhka fmfka' tneúka fuu m%ldYh ksjerÈh'

    B. The startDate attribute in the task relation is a derived attribute.
       iïnkaO;djfhys we;s startDate Wm,laIKh jHq;amkak l< (derived) Wm,laIKhls'
        A derived attribute is one that can be calculated from other attributes. The startDate in the task relation
        is not derived; it is a fundamental attribute recording when the task starts. Therefore, this statement is
        incorrect.
        jHq;amkak Wm,laIKhla hkq fjk;a Wm,laIK j,ska .Kkh l< yels tlls' ld¾h iïnkaO;djfha

       adviser iïnkaO;djfhys we;s adNIC hkak wfmalaIl (candidate) h;=rls'
        A candidate key is an attribute that can uniquely identify a record. Given that adNIC is a National
        Identity Card number, it is unique to each adviser and can serve as a candidate key. Therefore, this
        statement is correct.
        wfmalaIl h;=rla hkq jd¾;djla wkkHj y÷kd.; yels Wm,laIKhls' adNIC hkq cd;sl

   1. Consider the following relation:

          jd.a Í;suh jYfhka (syntactically) ksjerÈ fõ o?
          1)    select *.* from student
          2)    select all from student
          3)    select * from student
          4)    select stdNo.name from student
          5)    select stdNo; name from student

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2012 – 24 )
          Answer : 3 )

          Explanation / meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option/ úl,amh 1)
          Incorrect. The ** is not valid syntax in SQL. To select all columns, the correct syntax is SELECT * FROM
          table_name. / jerÈhs' SQL ys ** j,x.= jdlH LKavhla fkdfõ' ish¨u ;Sre f;aÍug" ksjerÈ jdlH
          LKavh SELECT * FROM table_name fõ'
          Option/ úl,amh 2)
          Incorrect. There is no keyword all for selecting columns in standard SQL. Correct syntax is SELECT *
          FROM student. / jerÈhs' iïu; SQL ys ;Sre f;aÍu i|yd all hk uQ, moh fkdue;' ksjerÈ jdlH
          LKavh SELECT * FROM student fõ'
          Option/ úl,amh 3)
          Correct. This is the standard and correct syntax to select all columns from a table. It returns all rows and
          all columns from the student table. / ksjerÈhs' j.=jlska ish¨u ;Sre f;aÍug fuh iïu; iy ksjerÈ
          jdlH LKavhhs' th YsIH j.=fjka ish¨u fma<s iy ish¨u ;Sre wdmiq ,nd fohs'
          Option/ úl,amh 4)
          Incorrect. In SQL, stdNo.name is invalid unless stdNo is an alias for a table and name is a column in that
          alias. Here, stdNo is just a column, so this syntax is not allowed. / jerÈhs' SQL ys" stdNo.name hkq
          j.=jla i|yd wkaj¾: kduhla jk w;r ku tu wkaj¾: kdufha ;Srejla fkdfõ kï ñi j,x.=
          fkdfõ' fuys§" stdNo hkq ;Srejla muKs" tneúka fuu jdlH LKavhg wjir ke;'
          Option/ úl,amh 5)
          Incorrect. The semicolon (;) is used to terminate a statement in SQL, not to separate column names. To
          select multiple columns, comma (,) is used. For example, SELECT stdNo, name FROM student.
          jerÈhs' ;Sre kï fjka lsÍug fkdj" SQL ys m%ldYhla wjika lsÍug semicolon (;) Ndú;d lrhs' nyq
          ;Sre f;aÍug" fldudj (,) Ndú;d lrhs' WodyrKhla f,i" SELECT stdNo, name FROM student.

          Therefore, option 3) is the correct answer.
          tneúka" 3) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ

   1. Consider the following two relations:
      my; olajd we;s iïnkaO;d fol i,lkak(
          student(stdNo, name)
          courseMarks(courseld, stdNo, marks)

          Which of the following SQL (Structured Query Language) statements on the above relations is
          syntactically correct?
          by; iïnkaO;d iïnkaOfhka my; olajd we;s SQL (Structured Query Language) j.ka;sj,ska jd.a
          Í;sj,g wkql+, j ksjerÈ l=ula o?

          1) select stdNo, marks from student, courseMarks
          2) select * from student and courseMarks
          3) select s.stdNo and c.marks from student s, courseMarks c
          4) select student.stdNo, courseMarks.marks from student, courseMarks
             where student.stdNo = courseMarks.stdNo
          5) select student.stdNo and courseMarks.marks from student and courseMarks
             where student.stdNo = courseMarks.stdNo

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2013 – 50 )
          Answer : 4 )

          Explanation / meyeos,s lsrsu
          The SQL query in Option 4 is syntactically correct and properly structured to retrieve data from two related
          tables. It selects student.stdNo and courseMarks.marks from both tables and uses a WHERE clause to join
          them based on the common field stdNo. This type of query is known as an inner join.
          úl,am 4 ys SQL úuiqu ldrl Í;Suh f,i ksjerÈ jk w;r wod< j.= follska o;a; ,nd .ekSu
          i|yd ksis f,i jHqy.; lr we;' th j.= foflkau student.stdNo iy courseMarks.marks f;dard
          .kakd w;r fmdÿ lafIa;% stdNo u; mokïj tAjd iïnkaO lsÍug WHERE j.ka;shla Ndú;d lrhs'
          fuu j¾.fha úuiqu wNHka;r iïnkaO lsÍula f,i ye¢kafõ'

          This query reflects best practices in SQL, where joins are used to combine related data from multiple
          tables. By using full table references (e.g., student.stdNo), the query remains unambiguous and precise,
          especially when the same field name exists in both tables.
          fuu úuiqu SQL ys fyd|u Ndú;hka ms<sìUq lrhs" tys§ nyq j.= j,ska wod< o;a; tAldnoaO lsÍug
          joins Ndú;d lrhs' iïmQ¾K j.= fhduq ^Wod( student.stdNo& Ndú;d lsÍfuka" úuiqu fkdmeyeÈ,s iy
          ksrjoHj mj;S" úfYaIfhka j.= foflysu tlu lafIa;% kduh mj;sk úg'

          Other options either use incorrect syntax (e.g., using and instead of a comma or join) or fail to include a
          proper joining condition. Therefore, Option 4 demonstrates the correct way to perform a table join in SQL,
          making it the right answer.
          wfkl=;a úl,am jerÈ jdlH LKavhla Ndú;d lrhs ^Wod( fldudjla fyda join fjkqjg iy Ndú;d lsÍu&
          fyda ksis iïnkaO ùfï fldkafoaishla we;=<;a lsÍug wfmdfydi;a fõ' tneúka" úl,am 4 SQL ys j.=
          iïnkaOhla isÿ lsÍug ksjerÈ l%uh fmkakqï lrhs" th ksjerÈ ms<s;=r njg m;a lrhs'

          Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.

   1. Which of the followings is correct with respect to the output of an SQL statement?
      SQL j.ka;shl m%;sodkh iïnkaOfhka my; i|yka l=ula ksjerÈ jkafka o?
          1) It generates an error if no data is available in the tables referred in the SQL statement.
             SQL j.ka;shlska fhduq jk j.= ;=< o;a; fkdmj;S kï túg fodaIhla W;amdokh fõ'
          2) It is always a table
             th iEu úgu j.=jls'
          3) The order of the attributes in output should be same as order of attributes in the table definition.
             m%;sodkfha Wm,elsj, ms<sfj< ^mámdáh& j.= ks¾jpkfha we;s Wm,elsj, ms<sfj< u úh hq;= h'
          4) Outputs cannot be produced if the primary keys are not defined.
             m%d:ñl h;=re w¾: olajd fkdue;s kï m%;sodk ,nd .; fkdyels h'
          5) Names of the attributes in the output should be the same as the names in the table definition.
             m%;sodkfha Wm,elsj, kdu j.= ks¾jpkfha we;s Wm,elsj, kdu u úh hq;= h'

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2016 – 46 )
          Answer : 2 )
          Explanation of each option / tla tla úl,amh meyeÈ,s lsÍu
          Option / úl,amh 1)
          Incorrect. If a table exists but contains no matching rows, SQL returns an empty result set, not an error.
          Errors occur only if the table doesn't exist or there’s a syntax or logic error. / jerÈhs' j.=jla mj;sk kuq;a
          .e,fmk fma<s fkdue;s kï" SQL fodaIhla fkdj ysia m%;sM, lÜg,hla wdmiq ,nd fohs' fodaI we;s
          jkafka j.=j fkdmj;S kï fyda jdlH LKavhla fyda ;d¾lsl fodaIhla ;sfí kï muKs'
          Option / úl,amh 2)
          Correct. SQL queries return result sets or a single row/column in virtual table-like structures. / ksjerÈhs'
          SQL úuiqï uÕska w:;H j.= jeks jHqyhka ;=< m%;sM, lÜg, fyda ;ks fma<shla$;Srejla ,nd foa'
          Option / úl,amh 3)
          Incorrect. The order of columns in the result depends on the SELECT clause, not on the table structure.
          Columns can be reordered freely in queries. / jerÈhs' m%;sM,fha ;Sre wkqms<sfj, j.= jHqyh u; fkdj
          SELECT j.ka;sh u; r|d mj;S' úuiqï ;=< ;Sre ksoyfia kej; weKjqï l< yelsh'
          Option / úl,amh 4)
          Incorrect. SQL queries work regardless of whether primary keys are defined. Primary keys are used for
          uniqueness and integrity, not for query output.
          jerÈhs' m%d:ñl h;=re w¾: olajd ;sfío hkak fkdi,ld SQL úuiqï l%shd lrhs' m%d:ñl h;=re úuiqï
          m%;sodkh i|yd fkdj woaú;ShNdjh iy wLKav;dj i|yd Ndú;d lrhs'
          Option / úl,amh 5)
          Incorrect. By default, the column names in the output are the same as in the table, but they can be changed
          using AS (ex: SELECT name AS student_name) or derived (ex: SELECT COUNT(*) AS total).
          jerÈhs' fmrksñfhka" m%;sodkfha we;s ;Sre kï j.=fõ we;s wdldrhgu fõ" kuq;a tAjd AS ^Wod(
          SELECT name AS student_name& fyda jHq;amkak ^Wod( SELECT COUNT(*) AS total& Ndú;fhka
          fjkia l< yel'

 2. The length of an attribute defined using a Data Definition Language (DDL) statement is a/an
    ................. constraint.
    o;a; ks¾jpk NdId(DDL) j.ka;shla Ndú;fhka w¾: olajd we;s Wm,elshl È.(length) ....................
    ixfrdaOlhla fõ'
    Which of the followings is the most appropriate to fill the blank in the above statement?
    my; i|yka l=ula by; j.ka;sfhys we;s ysia;ek msrùu i|yd jvd;a iqÿiq jkafka o?

    1) primary key 2) foreign key                 3) null value  4) domain           5) application
       m%d:ñl h;=re   wd.ka;=l h;=re                 wNsY=kH w.h    jiï                 fhÿï

                                                                                                 ( 2016 – 47 )
    Answer : 4 )

    Explanation of each option / tla tla úl,amh meyeÈ,s lsÍu

    Option / úl,amh 1)
    Primary key ensures uniqueness of a column. It is not related to attribute length.
    m%d:ñl h;=r ;Srejl wkkH;djh iy;sl lrhs' th Wm,laIK È.g iïnkaO fkdfõ'

    Option / úl,amh 2)
    Foreign key maintains referential integrity. It doesn’t define length.
    wd.ka;=l h;=r fhduq wLKav;dj mj;ajd .kS' th È. ks¾jpkh fkdlrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 3)
    Null value indicates whether a column can have NULLs. It is not a length constraint.
    Y=kH w.h uÕska ;Srejlg NULL ;sìh yelso hkak olajhs' th È. iïndOlhla fkdfõ'

    Option / úl,amh 4)
    A domain constraint defines the permissible values for a given attribute. Length, data type, and format
    are part of domain constraints.
    jiï iSudjla § we;s .=Kdx.hla i|yd wjir ,;a w.hka ks¾jpkh lrhs' È." o;a; j¾.h iy
    wdlD;sh jiï iïndOlhkays fldgils'

    Example: VARCHAR(20) defines the maximum length of a string → a domain constraint.
    WodyrKh( VARCHAR(20) string tll Wmßu È. → jiï iïndOlhla ks¾jpkh lrhs'

    Option / úl,amh 5)
    Application refers to business logic outside the database, not DDL level constraints.
    fhÿu DDL uÜgfï iïndOlhka fkdj o;a; iuqodfhka msg; jHdmdr ;¾lkhg fhduq fõ'

    Therefore, option 4) is the correct answer.
    tneúka" 4) úl,amh ksjerÈ ms<s;=r fõ'

    zscore in the above table to 2.1?

    j;auka zscore tl fkdi,ld" fuh j.=fõ iEu fma<shlau hdj;ald,Sk lrhs'

    In SQL,SQL statements refer to SQL queries. So,by that definition, the correct answer should be option 1.

    1 úh hq;=h'

    But in some cases, ‘SQL statements’ can be used to address any valid SQL command such as INSERT,
    UPDATE, SET, CREATE etc. It includes all types of commands such as Data Query Language, Data
    Manipulation Language, Data Definition Language, Data Control Language etc. So, the number of SQL
    statements used in the given query is 2. So, by this definition we can take option 2) as the correct answer.
    kuq;a iuyr wjia:dj,§" INSERT" UPDATE" SET" CREATE jeks ´kEu j,x.= SQL úOdkhla
    wduka;%Kh lsÍug ‘SQL statements’ hkak Ndú;d l< yelsh' thg o;a; úuiqï NdIdj" o;a; yeisrùfï

    tneúka" § we;s úuiqfï Ndú;d lr we;s SQL m%ldYk .Kk 2 ls' tneúka" fuu w¾: oelaùu wkqj
    wmg úl,amh 2 ksjerÈ ms<s;=r f,i .; yelsh'

    Marks were given to both the answers, depending on the definition.
    w¾: oelaùu wkqj ms<s;=re folgu ,l=Kq ,nd § we;'

    To update all values of the attribute zscore in the table to 2.1 with minimum SQL statements, we can use
    the following SQL statement
    j.=fõ we;s zscore Wm,laIKfha ish¨u w.hka wju SQL m%ldYk iys;j 2.1 fj; hdj;ald,Sk lsÍug"

 2. Which of the following SQL commands is not available in the Data Manipulation Language (DML)?
    o;a; yeiqreï NdIdfjys (DML) fkdue;s SQL úOdkhla jkafka my; l=ula o?

    1) CREATE         2) DELETE         3) INSERT          4) SELECT         5) UPDATE

                                                                                          ( 2019 – 18 )
    Answer : 1)

    CREATE is not a DML command because it defines database structures rather than manipulating data.
    CREATE hkq DML úOdkhla fkdfõ" ukao th o;a; yeisrùug jvd o;a; iuqod jHqyhka ks¾jpkh
    lrhs'

    SQL is divided into several categories, and Data Manipulation Language (DML) includes commands that
    modify or retrieve data in a database. The four main DML commands are,
    SQL ldKav lsysmhlg fnod we;s w;r" o;a; yeisrùfï NdIdj DML o;a; .nvdjl o;a; fjkia
    lrk fyda ,nd .kakd úOdk we;=<;a fõ' m%Odk DML úOdk y;r jkafka"

    •   SELECT – Retrieves data. o;a; ,nd .kshs'
    •   INSERT – Adds new records. kj jd¾;d tl;= lrhs'
    •   UPDATE – Modifies existing records. mj;sk jd¾;d fjkia lrhs'
    •   DELETE – Removes records. jd¾;d bj;a lrhs'

    However, CREATE is not a DML command. It belongs to the Data Definition Language (DDL), which is
    used for defining and modifying database structures (tables, schemas, etc.).
    flfia fj;;a" CREATE hkq DML úOdkhla fkdfõ' th o;a; iuqod jHqyhka ^j.=" fhdackd l%u" wdÈh&
    ks¾jpkh lsÍu iy fjkia lsÍu i|yd Ndú;d lrk o;a; ks¾jpk NdIdjg (DDL) wh;a fõ'

                                     2020 SQL MCQ MARKING

   1. What is the correct SQL statement to delete a database called 'ALdb'?
      'ALdb' f,i ye¢kafjk o;a; iuqodh (database) uldoeóu (delete) i|yd ksjerÈ SQL m%ldYh l=ulao?

          1) delete ALdb;                                                2) delete database ALdb;                                       3) drop ALdb;
          4) drop database ALdb;                                         5) remove database ALdb;

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2020 – 26 )
          Answer : 4 )

          In SQL, databases can be managed using specific Data Definition Language (DDL) commands. To delete
          a database, the correct command is DROP DATABASE.
          SQL ys" o;a; iuqodhka úfYaIs; o;a; ks¾jpk NdIdj (DDL) úOdk Ndú;fhka l<ukdlrKh l<
          yel' o;a; iuqodhla uld oeóu i|yd ksjerÈ úOdkh jkafka DROP DATABASE hkakhs'

          Let’s explore the given options.
          ,nd § we;s úl,am i,ld n,uq'

          1) delete ALdb;
             Incorrect. The DELETE command in SQL is used to delete rows from a table, not to delete databases.
             jerÈ fõ' SQL ys we;s DELETE úOdkh Ndú;d lrkafka j.=jlska fma<s uld oeóug ñi o;a;
             iuqodhka uld oeóug fkdfõ'

          2) delete database ALdb;
             Incorrect . DELETE command is not used for deleting databases; it’s used for deleting records within
             tables.
             jerÈ fõ' o;a; iuqodhka uld oeóu i|yd DELETE úOdkh Ndú;d fkdfõ. th j.= ;=< jd¾;d
             uld oeóug Ndú;d lrhs'

          3) drop ALdb;
             This is incomplete. SQL requires the full DROP DATABASE statement to specify what exactly
             is being dropped
             fuh wiïmQ¾Khs' SQL g ksjerÈju DROP lrkafka hkak i|yka lsÍug iïmQ¾K DROP
             DATABASE m%ldYh wjYH fõ

          4) drop database ALdb;
             Correct. The DROP DATABASE command completely removes the database named 'ALdb'
             and all of its associated data, including tables, views, procedures, and anything else stored in the
             database.
             ksjerÈ fõ' DROP DATABASE úOdkh uÕska 'ALdb' kï o;a; iuqodh iy j.=" o¾Ykh" l%shd
             mámdá iy o;a; iuqodfha .nvd lr we;s fjk;a ´kEu fohla we;=¿j thg iïnkaO ish¨u
             o;a; iïmQ¾Kfhkau bj;a lrhs'

          5) remove database ALdb;
             Incorrect. REMOVE is not a valid SQL keyword for database management.
             jerÈ fõ' REMOVE hkq o;a; iuqod l<ukdlrKh i|yd j,x.= SQL uQ, mohla fkdfõ'

          Therefore, the correct answer is, 4).
          tneúka, ksjerÈ ms<s;=r" 4) fõ'

 2. Consider the following SQL statement:
    my; oelafjk SQL m%ldYh i,lkak'
    Alter table subject add primary key (Subject_Id);
    Which of the following is incorrect about the above SQL statement?

    1) It adds a primary key constraint to the table named subject.
       th subject kï jk j.=jg m%d:ñl h;=re ixfrdaOlhla tl;= lrhs'
    2) The table named subject should already exist.
       subject kñka jk j.=j fï jk úg mej;sh hq;= h'
    3) The field Subject_Id should not be null.
       Subject_Id kï lafIa;%h wNsY=kH fkdúh hq;=h'
    4) A table named subject is created with a primary key named Subject_ld.
       th Subject_Id kï jQ m%d:ñl h;=rla iys; subject kï jQ j.=jla ks¾udKh lrhs'
    5) The values of the field Subject_Id should not be repeated in subject table.
       subject j.=fjys Subject_Id kï lafIa;%fhys we;s w.hka mqK¾lrKh (repeat) úh fkdyel'

                                                                                                ( 2020 – 29 )
    Answer : 4 )
    Let’s consider the given statements about the SQL statement above.
    by; SQL m%ldYh ms<sn|j ,nd § we;s m%ldY i,ld n,uq'

    1) It adds a primary key constraint to the table named subject.
       th subject kï jk j.=jg m%d:ñl h;=re ixfrdaOlhla tl;= lrhs'
        Correct: The ALTER TABLE statement is used to add a primary key constraint to the existing table
        subject on the column Subject_Id
        ksjerÈ fõ' ALTER TABLE m%ldYh Subject_Id ;Srefõ mj;sk subject j.=jg m%d:ñl h;=re
        ixfrdaOlhla tla lsÍug Ndú;d lrhs

    2) The table named subject should already exist.
       subject kñka jk j.=j fï jk úg mej;sh hq;= h'
        Correct: The ALTER TABLE command modifies an existing table, so table subject must already exist.
        ksjerÈ fõ' ALTER TABLE úOdkh oekg mj;sk j.=jla fjkia lrhs" tneúka subject j.=j
        oekgu;a mej;sh hq;=h'

    3) The field Subject_Id should not be null
       Subject_Id kï lafIa;%h wNsY=kH (null) fkdúh hq;= h'
        Correct: When a primary key is added, the field must have the NOT NULL constraint, meaning it
        cannot contain null values.
        ksjerÈ fõ' m%d:ñl h;=rla tla l< úg" lafIa;%hg NOT NULL iSudj ;sìh hq;=h" tkï tys Y=kH
        w.hka wvx.= úh fkdyel'

    4) A table named subject is created with a primary key named Subject_ld
       th Subject_Id kï jQ m%d:ñl h;=rla iys; subject kï jQ j.=jla ks¾udKh lrhs'
        Incorrect: The SQL statement does’nt create table; it modifies an existing one by adding primary key.
        jerÈ fõ' SQL m%ldYh j.=jla ks¾udKh fkdlrhs th m%d:ñl h;=rla tl;= lsÍfuka mj;sk tlla
        fjkia lrhs'

    5) The values of the field Subject_Id should not be repeated in subject table.
       subject j.=fjys Subject_Id kï lafIa;%fhys we;s w.hka mqK¾lrKh (repeat) úh fkdyel'
        Correct: A primary key enforces uniqueness, so Subject_Id values must not be repeated.
        ksjerÈ fõ' m%d:ñl h;=rla wkkH úh hq;=h" tneúka Subject_Id w.hka mqkrdj¾;kh fkdúh hq;=h'

                                      2021 SQL MCQ MARKING

   •      Consider the following Results and Subjects tables to answer questions from 01 to 04:
          m%Yak wxl 01 isg 04 f;la ms<s;=re §ug my; Results iy Subjects j.= i,lkak'

          Results
             StudentNo                NIC                              FirstName                              SubjectID                        Grade
               S1234               986888457V                            Nilam                                  ENG                              B
               S1447               992562321V                           Praveena                                PHY                             C
               S1234               986888457V                            Nilam                                  ACC                             A
               S1323               900251452V                            Thilan                                 ENG                              S
               S1323               900251452V                            Thilan                                 ACC                              B
          Subjects
                  SubjectID                   SubjectName
                    ENG                         English
                    PHY                         Physics
                    ECO                        Economics
                    ACC                       Accountancy

   1. What is the correct SQL statement to retrieve the values of attributes StudentNo, SubjectName and
      Grade? / StudentNo, SubjectName iy Grade hk Wm,elsj, w.hhka ,nd.ekSug wod< SQL
      j.ka;sh my; ljrla o?
          1) SELECT Results.StudentNo, Subjects.SubjectName, Results.Grade FROM Results
             INNER JOIN ON Results.SubjectID = Subjects.SubjectID;
          2) SELECT Results.StudentNo, Subjects.SubjectName, Results.Grade FROM Results
             INNER JOIN Results.SubjectID = Subjects. SubjectID;
          3) SELECT Results.StudentNo, Subjects.SubjectName, Results.Grade FROM Results

   1. Which of the following will exchange of ‘Mahawa’ in the ‘City’ attribute of USER in relation to ‘Maho’?
      my; ljrla u.ska USER iïnkaOfha ‘City’ Wm,laIKfha we;s ish¨ ‘Mahawa’ f,i we;s ;eka ‘Maho’
      f,i fjkia lrhs o@
          1)    MODIFY USER SET City = ‘Maho’ WHERE City = ‘Mahawa’;
          2)    MODIFY USER SET City = ‘Mahawa’ INTO City = ‘Maho’;
          3)    UPDATE USER SET City = ‘Mahawa’ INTO City = ‘Maho’;
          4)    UPDATE USER SET City = ‘Maho’ WHERE City = ‘Mahawa’;
          5)    UPDATE USER SET City = ‘Maho’ WHERE City != ‘Mahawa’;

                                                                                                                                                         ( 2023 – 36 )
          Answer : 4 )

          Let’s go through the options given / ,nd § we;s úl,am yryd huq'

          Option / úl,amh 1)
          Incorrect. MODIFY is not a valid SQL keyword for updating records.
          jerÈ fõ' MODIFY hkq jd¾;d hdj;ald,Sk lsÍu i|yd j,x.= SQL uQ, mohla fkdfõ'

          Option/ úl,amh 2)
          Incorrect. The use of MODIFY and INTO is incorrect in this context.
          jerÈ fõ' fuys MODIFY iy INTO Ndú;h jerÈh'

          Option/ úl,amh 3)
          Incorrect. The INTO clause is not used in an UPDATE statement.
          jerÈ fõ' INTO j.ka;sh UPDATE lsÍfï m%ldYhl Ndú;d fkdfõ'

          Option/ úl,amh 4)
          Correct. The UPDATE command is used to modify existing records. The SET keyword is used to specify
          the new value for the City column, and the WHERE clause ensures that only records with 'Mahawa' in the
          City column are updated to 'Maho'.
          ksjerÈhs' UPDATE úOdkh oekg mj;sk jd¾;d fjkia lsÍug Ndú;d lrhs' k.r ;Srej i|yd kj
          w.h kshu lsÍug SET uQ, moh Ndú;d lrk w;r" WHERE j.ka;sh k.rfha ;Srefõ 'Mahawa'
          iys; jd¾;d muKla 'Maho' f,i hdj;ald,Sk lrk nj iy;sl lrhs'

          Option/ úl,amh 5)
          Incorrect. This statement will update all records where the City is not 'Mahawa', setting those cities to
          'Maho'. This is not the intended behavior, as it will change cities other than 'Mahawa'.
          jerÈ fõ' fuu m%ldYh k.rh 'Mahawa' fkdjk ish¨u jd¾;d hdj;ald,Sk lr tu k.r 'Maho' f,i
          ilihs' th 'Mahawa' yer wfkl=;a k.r fjkia lrk neúka fuh wfmalaIs; yeisÍu fkdfõ'

          Therefore, the correct answer is 4).

 2. Which of the following lists the given SQL statement clauses in the correct order?
    § we;s SQL m%ldY mo ksjerÈj fm< fm<.iajd we;af;a ljrl o@

     1)   SELECT,     FROM,           WHERE,             GROUP BY,     HAVING
     2)   SELECT,     GROUP BY,       HAVING,            FROM,         WHERE
     3)   SELECT,     HAVING,         FROM,              WHERE,        GROUP BY
     4)   SELECT,     WHERE,          GROUP BY,          HAVING,       FROM
     5)   SELECT,     WHERE,          HAVING,            GROUP BY,     FROM

                                                                                                  ( 2023 – 37 )
     Answer : 1 )

     In SQL, the correct order of clauses in a SELECT statement is crucial for the query to function properly.
     The correct order is as follows,
     SQL ys" úuiqu ksis f,i l%shd;aul ùug SELECT m%ldYhl j.ka;sj, ksjerÈ wkqms<sfj, b;d jeo.;a
     fõ' ksjerÈ wkqms<sfj, my; mßÈ fõ"

     1    SELECT
          This clause specifies which columns you want to retrieve. It comes first.
          fuu j.ka;sh Tng ,nd .ekSug wjYH ;Sre kshu lrhs' th uq,skau meñfKa'

     2    FROM
          This clause specifies the table from which to retrieve the data. It follows the SELECT clause.
          fuu j.ka;sh o;a; ,nd .; hq;= j.=j kshu lrhs' th SELECT j.ka;sh wkq.ukh lrhs'

     3    WHERE
          This clause is used to filter records based on specified conditions. It comes after FROM.
          fuu j.ka;sh ksYaÑ; fldkafoais u; mokïj jd¾;d f;aÍug' Ndú;d lrhs' th FROM g miqj
          fhfoa'

     4    GROUP BY
          This clause groups rows that have the same values into summary rows, like finding the sum or average.
          It follows WHERE if used.
          fuu j.ka;sh tl;=j fyda idudkHh fidhd .ekSu jeks iudk w.hka we;s fma<s idrdxY fma<sj,g
          ldKav lrhs' th WHERE g miqj fhfoa'

     5    HAVING
